+package api
+
+import (
+	"context"
+	"sync"
+	"time"
+)
+
+// GrantLimiter enforces the token-issuing endpoint's own failed-grant
+// rate limiting and progressive backoff — handoff-03-auth.md v3:
+// "Token-endpoint brute-force protection, separate from the resource-API
+// rate limits ... failed-grant rate limiting and progressive backoff per
+// client_id and per source IP on the token-issuing endpoint itself, plus
+// alerting on a sustained run of failed grants against one client_id."
+//
+// This is genuinely separate from RateLimiter (ratelimit.go): that type
+// gates the resource API by an already-authenticated sub, at a fixed
+// per-minute rate; this one gates the endpoint that issues the
+// credential in the first place, keyed independently by client_id AND
+// by source IP — a distributed brute force (many IPs, one client_id)
+// and a single-source credential-stuffing run (one IP, many client_ids)
+// are both attack shapes this is meant to slow down, and either key
+// exceeding its own backoff blocks the attempt.
+//
+// Not wired to a live endpoint by this story: /auth/token's real grant
+// logic is LT-51's scope (this story's non-goals). This is the
+// ready-to-use component LT-51 constructs and calls from the real
+// handler — building it now, tested in isolation, means LT-51 doesn't
+// also have to design a backoff/alerting scheme under its own deadline.
+type GrantLimiter interface {
+	// Allow reports whether a grant attempt for clientID from sourceIP
+	// may proceed right now — false while either key is in backoff.
+	Allow(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string) (bool, error)
+	// RecordFailure records a failed grant attempt for both clientID and
+	// sourceIP, advancing each key's backoff state independently.
+	// Crossing the alert threshold for a given client_id (not source IP
+	// — the requirement names client_id specifically) fires alert
+	// exactly once per crossing, not once per subsequent failure.
+	RecordFailure(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string)
+	// RecordSuccess clears backoff state for both clientID and
+	// sourceIP — a successful grant resets the count, standard
+	// exponential-backoff practice: this deters sustained brute force,
+	// it does not permanently degrade a legitimate client that mistyped
+	// a secret once.
+	RecordSuccess(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string)
+}
+
+// grantBackoffState is one key's (a client_id's, or a source IP's)
+// consecutive-failure bookkeeping.
+type grantBackoffState struct {
+	consecutiveFailures int
+	blockedUntil        time.Time
+	alerted             bool
+}
+
+// InProcessGrantLimiter is an in-process implementation. As with
+// RateLimiter/TouchCounter's in-process implementations, a shared store
+// is the multi-instance replacement behind the same interface — the
+// mechanism is this track's call, per handoff-03-auth.md v3.
+//
+// Both maps are swept periodically (see sweepLocked) rather than left to
+// grow forever: this component's own doc comment names "a distributed
+// brute force (many IPs, one client_id)" as a defended attack shape, and
+// that is exactly the traffic pattern that would otherwise grow
+// bySourceIP without bound — every distinct attacking IP earns a
+// permanent entry that only a matching RecordSuccess would remove, which
+// an attacking IP by definition never triggers (Nolan Reyes, PR #30
+// review — the same unbounded-growth shape an earlier PR's TouchCounter
+// review already found and fixed once).
+type InProcessGrantLimiter struct {
+	mu             sync.Mutex
+	byClientID     map[string]*grantBackoffState
+	bySourceIP     map[string]*grantBackoffState
+	baseBackoff    time.Duration
+	maxBackoff     time.Duration
+	alertThreshold int
+	alertFn        func(clientID string, consecutiveFailures int)
+	now            func() time.Time
+	lastSweep      time.Time
+}
+
+// grantSweepInterval and grantEntryTTL bound how long a key's state
+// survives with no further failures against it. A blocked key is never
+// swept before its own backoff has elapsed (sweeping mid-block would
Shell cwd was reset to /Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID/01-product-industry-research-design

3:Status: **v6** — fixes the JWKS path/URL and cache/refresh guidance (below), ruled so LT-51 implements to this doc rather than the doc following PR #30's already-shipped code. v5's Authorizer stand-in and cumulative-cap answers are unchanged. Receiver: Renata Cole, `../03-engineering-delivery/`. Written so 03 should be able to scaffold and build the middleware without re-deriving the reasoning in `api-auth-design.md`, `connector-security.md`, or the S3/S4/S8 decision records. Full reasoning, if wanted: `api-auth-design.md`, `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`.
27:**Public-key distribution for the verifier (enforcement site, your call was open, ruled here): JWKS served by the issuer Deployment, reachable only in-cluster via its own `ClusterIP` Service — no Ingress route, never publicly reachable.** Not a mounted public-key file: JWKS survives key rotation without redeploying the verifying Deployment's config, since the verifier fetches by `kid` and only needs to know the issuer Service's in-cluster DNS name (`<issuer-service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local`), not track individual key files. In-cluster-only because this token scheme's only consumers are `api-service` and `idp-connector`, both already inside the cluster network; there's no reason to add a new publicly-reachable route on a shared production cluster (`threat-model.md` Assumption 7) for a key set that only internal services ever need.
29:**Path and URL (ruled 2026-09-13, per PR #30/LT-51 — fixed now so LT-51 implements to the doc, not the other way around):** `/.well-known/jwks.json`, the OIDC-discovery convention, served only when `APP_MODE=issuer`. Live at `AUTH_JWKS_URL=http://api-service-issuer.loginid-takehome.svc.cluster.local/.well-known/jwks.json`, already wired by Theo.
31:**Cache/refresh guidance:** the issuer may set a normal `Cache-Control: public, max-age=<refresh interval>` on the response as good HTTP practice, but it's advisory only — **the verifier's own staleness policy is authoritative, not the issuer's cache headers.** That policy is already built (PR #30, Tomasz's re-check confirmed it closed): max staleness = 3× the normal refresh TTL, fail closed (`ErrJWKSStale`, distinct `AuditJWKSUnavailable` event) past that bound rather than serving indefinitely-stale keys; an unknown `kid` triggers one out-of-band refetch, rate-limited **per-`kid`** (not one global gate — a global gate lets an unauthenticated caller with a garbage `kid` exhaust the shared refetch slot for free, since `kid` lookup runs before signature verification; keying per-`kid` closes that without reopening the endpoint-hammering concern the rate limit exists to prevent).
50:- **Key retrieval:** middleware verifies with the **public** key only (JWKS or a fetched public key — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3). The private signing key never touches the verifying `api-service` Deployment; it lives with the token-issuing function, which is a mode of the same `api-service` image but runs as its **own Deployment** (`APP_MODE=issuer`, its own ServiceAccount), not the same Deployment doing both (corrected in `handoff-04-secrets.md` Assumption 2 and row #2, F13 — RBAC on a Secret's `get` verb doesn't stop a volume already mounted into a shared Deployment's containers, so the separation has to be a second Deployment, not RBAC alone). This is a second `APP_MODE` value for you to wire alongside your existing config surface.

1	# LT-41 refinement — S8: connector `/auth` + `/identity` (Q3)
2	
3	**Product Owner:** Naomi Voss (01). **Research team:** Imogen Hale, Desmond Okafor, Tobias Lindqvist. **Engineering:** Renata Cole (03). **Source:** `03-engineering-delivery/backlog.md` S8 (LT-41); `02-ai-security-architecture/connector-security.md` (including its attack-tree table), `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`, `handoff-03-auth.md` v6.
4	
5	**Who did the work:** Refined by Naomi Voss (PO); claims check Desmond Okafor; objection Tobias Lindqvist; engineering counterpart Renata Cole.
6	
7	## Why this one, now
8	
9	Q3 (the third assignment question) has no code yet, and it's genuinely independent of LT-51's in-flight work: the connector only *validates* a `connector:identity-lookup`-scoped token presented to it — it doesn't need to know how `api-service` obtains that token in the first place (that's LT-33, 02's still-open story, on `api-service`'s outbound side). Pulling this now keeps refinement from being the bottleneck while Renata and Theo build LT-51.
10	
11	**Checked the dependency graph rather than assuming:** LT-33 (02, `handoff-04-secrets.md` row 10 + `api-service`'s client credential) depends in part on "03's connector-client implementation," but that's the *outbound caller* inside `api-service`, not this story — this story is `cmd/idp-connector` itself, the inbound side. LT-41 can be built, refined, and merged without LT-33 closing first; LT-33 is what wires `api-service` to call it correctly, a separate piece.
12	
13	## Correction to `backlog.md`'s own S8 text, found before drafting
14	
15	**Two real controls from `connector-security.md`'s attack-tree table (S3 red/blue, already run and ruled) are missing from `backlog.md`'s S8 acceptance criteria entirely — not stale, just never transcribed down from the design doc into the story.** The table is the authoritative, already-ratified source; the story text predates or simply didn't carry two of its concrete requirements forward:
16	
17	1. **Leaf 1 (connection-reuse token leak):** "fresh, isolated auth header constructed per outbound request, never inherited from connection reuse" — a pooled HTTP client that doesn't reset `Authorization` per call can attach one request's token to another, even with no cache. `backlog.md`'s S8 only names `context.WithTimeout` + bounded pool + backoff from `decisions/go-layout-debate.md`; it never named this specific, checkable requirement.
18	2. **Leaf 5 (credential-stuffing oracle):** rate limiting on outbound vendor calls, keyed per calling-API-client **and** per source IP, with a stricter threshold specifically on vendor-auth **failure ratio** (not just volume — credential stuffing shows as a high failure ratio before high volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted. `backlog.md`'s S8 has no rate-limiting criterion for outbound vendor calls at all.
19	
20	Both are added below as acceptance criteria, since the attack-tree table already ruled on them (Residual L on both, after blue's response) — this refinement is transcribing an already-ratified control, not inventing a new one.
21	
22	## Acceptance criteria (restated from `backlog.md` S8, plus the two additions above)
23	
24	- [ ] Implements 02's token-lifecycle spec (`connector-security.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`) without re-litigating the no-cache-by-default posture.
25	- [ ] No vendor token is persisted or cached across requests by default; if a future exception is ever justified it requires re-review, not a silent default change — enforcement site: no storage/cache call in the token-fetch path.
26	- [ ] Every outbound vendor call sets `context.WithTimeout`; the outbound HTTP client uses a bounded connection pool; token-refresh retries back off rather than tight-looping — enforcement site: the HTTP client construction plus a test asserting a timeout fires.
27	- [ ] **Added (attack-tree leaf 1):** a fresh, isolated `Authorization` header is constructed per outbound vendor request — never inherited from HTTP connection/keep-alive reuse. Enforcement site: a test making two sequential vendor calls with two different presented tokens through the same pooled client, asserting the second call's outbound header matches its own token, not the first call's.
28	- [ ] **Added (attack-tree leaf 5):** outbound vendor calls are rate-limited keyed per calling-API-client identity **and** per source IP independently, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth failure ratio specifically (not just raw volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted. Enforcement site: the rate-limit/backoff component plus a test asserting a high-failure-ratio pattern trips the stricter threshold before a high-volume-but-mostly-successful pattern would.
29	- [ ] The connector's own `/auth`/`/identity` handlers validate an inbound JWT with `aud: idp-connector-service` and `scope: connector:identity-lookup`, distinct from `api-service`'s audience — enforcement site: this handler's own middleware instance, with a test asserting a `loginid-api-service`-audience token is rejected here.
30	- [ ] `/identity` accepts the vendor token as a request header (not a body field, since the assignment's fixed `{"phone","name"}` body has no credential field), fails closed with a generic error that doesn't distinguish "no token" from "bad token" from "vendor rejected it" (same user-enumeration discipline as elsewhere in this project) — per `connector-security.md`'s F3 resolution.
31	- [ ] The token variable is scoped to the narrowest function possible and never passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function itself — per attack-tree leaf 4's control (accepted that Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing, but the exposure window is narrowed to one function call, not eliminated).
32	- [ ] Never logs, in any environment or error path: the end-user's vendor password, any vendor `access_token` or full `Authorization` header value, or raw vendor error response bodies (per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list) — enforcement site: log-call review against that list, plus confirming the token field is excluded from any generic request/response dump used in panic traces or debug output, not only application logs (leaf 4's "same discipline as application logs" extension).
33	
34	## Non-goals
35	
36	- No implementation of `api-service`'s outbound call to this connector, or how `api-service` obtains its own `connector:identity-lookup` token — that's LT-33 (02, still open), a separate story.
37	- No real ABC/XYZ vendor integration — a stubbed vendor per the assignment's generic framing; this story's job is the connector's own contract and security properties, not a specific vendor's API quirks.
38	- No change to `handoff-03-auth.md`'s scope vocabulary or claim scheme — already fixed (v6).
39	- No shared rate-limit store/edge mechanism — attack-tree leaf 5 names that as 04's mechanism if enforced at a gateway; this story's own in-process rate limiting is sufficient for this take-home's scale, matching the pattern already used for `RateLimiter`/`TouchCounter`/`GrantLimiter`.
40	
41	## Flagged gap for the joint LT-34/39/40/51 live-URL review script (not this story's own scope)
42	
43	**Per team-lead's request to update the joint review script** (mint a token, create a profile via whatever write path exists, search it masked, read it full with a `reason_code`, hit the 429 cap): **checked directly — there is no write/create path anywhere in `internal/api`.** `backlog.md`'s S6 (LT-39) only implements search/retrieve (Q2's explicit scope); no `POST /profiles` handler exists, and none is currently planned as a story. The migration `Job`'s `shared/` directory only seeds the `auth_method` lookup row (per `migration-approach.md`), not `user_profile`/`user_credential` test data — there's no seeding mechanism for a reviewable profile either.
44	
45	**This is a real gap in the joint review script's own plan, not something this story can quietly work around.** Two options, neither decided here: (a) a small, explicit "seed one test profile" step — either a one-off manual `INSERT` against the deployed Postgres instance (out-of-band, like the credential bootstraps) for review purposes only, clearly not a production data-entry path, or (b) a minimal `POST /profiles` handler as a new, small story if profile creation is expected to exist at all for a complete Q1/Q2 demonstration. Flagging both to team-lead rather than picking one — this is a product/scope decision (does the submission need a write path at all?), not an engineering detail.

48	
49	1. Confirm the fresh-header-per-request test (leaf 1) exists and passes.
50	2. Confirm the failure-ratio rate-limit test (leaf 5) exists and passes.
51	3. Confirm the connector's inbound middleware rejects a `loginid-api-service`-audience token (wrong audience for this service).
52	4. Confirm `/identity`'s generic failure response doesn't distinguish no-token/bad-token/vendor-rejected cases.
53	5. Read the vendor-call code path directly: confirm the token variable's scope doesn't escape the vendor-call function, and confirm no log call anywhere references the token, the vendor password, or a raw vendor error body.
54	6. Once deployed (this connector has no public route — reachable only from `api-service` via the existing `NetworkPolicy`): no direct live-URL check is possible for this story on its own; its behavior is exercised indirectly once `api-service`'s outbound call (LT-33) actually invokes it as part of the onboarding flow. Not part of the LT-34/39/40/51 joint review, which only covers Q1/Q2.

## Attack-tree table — connector token lifecycle (S3 red/blue, 2026-09-12)

Full positions, rebuttals, and ruling: `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`. Table shape per `planning-approach.md` Appendix A.

| # | Leaf (attack path) | Precondition | Likelihood | Impact | Blue response: Control *or* Accepted risk | Owner | Residual | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Cache-key confusion → cross-tenant token/PII leak via request-derived cache key | Persistent cache (eliminated by default posture below) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default (Felix Adebayo, alternatives seat) — no persistent cache means no key to collide on. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, key must derive from immutable vendor-issued ids, never request-supplied `name`/`phone`. **Control (this run, Tomasz's rebuttal):** the connection-reuse variant of this leaf survives even with no cache — a pooled HTTP client that doesn't reset `Authorization` per call can attach one request's token to another. Closed with a required control: fresh, isolated auth header constructed per outbound request, never inherited from connection reuse. | 03 (client construction; cache-key derivation if exception ever opened) | **L** — storage variant eliminated; connection-reuse variant closed by an explicit, checkable implementation requirement | OWASP API8:2023; judgment (connection-reuse variant) |
| 2 | Fail-open on KMS unavailability during a caching window | Persistent cache with KMS-backed encryption at rest (eliminated by default posture) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default removes the write-time KMS dependency from the hot path. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, KMS-unreachable fails closed to "no cache, fetch live," never to an unencrypted write. Tomasz conceded this leaf outright — no hole found. | 03/04 (KMS mechanism is 04's) | **L** | NIST SP 800-57 |
| 3 | Vendor token replay — no proof-of-possession/session binding once decrypted for use | Token exfiltrated by any other means within its exposure window | M | H | **Accepted risk.** Bearer tokens under RFC 6750 are possession-equals-authorization by design; PoP/binding (DPoP, RFC 9449; mTLS-bound tokens, RFC 8705) needs vendor-side support this project cannot assume for a generic ABC/XYZ vendor the assignment never specifies. Acceptable *for this system* because the token is never cached or persisted on either side of the exchange — it exists only in the caller's possession for the brief span between our `/auth` and `/identity` calls, and only inside the connector's `/identity` handler for that single request, on the connector's side (**updated for F3's caller-holds-the-token model, seam re-run regression fix** — the original justification described the connector itself fetching and using the token in one function call; the exposure window is now bounded by the same no-cache/no-persist discipline applied across two parties instead of inside one function). Retrying `/identity` with a stale token is the caller's decision, not the connector's own retry-with-reuse — the connector treats each presented token as freshly supplied and never retries the vendor call on its own initiative with a token it already knows is invalid. | — (re-review trigger: a PoP-capable vendor surfacing in 01's research) | **M** — accepted, not eliminated; a real vendor-side constraint, not a design gap, but still "possession = authority" for the token's lifetime | RFC 6750 §5; RFC 9700 §2.5 |
| 4 | Process-memory/crash-dump exposure of the plaintext token before encryption | Attacker has read access to a crash artifact or swap on the host | L | M | **Control:** token field excluded from any generic request/response dump used in error handling (panic traces, debug dumps — same discipline as application logs); token variable scoped to the narrowest function possible, never passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function. **Residual accepted:** Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing without unsafe/cgo, so a crash dump taken mid-call could still capture it; acceptable given the window narrowed from a cache TTL to one function call (Felix's alternatives turn). Core-dump suppression at the runtime level is a 04 mechanism, requirement recorded, not resolved here. | 03 (code discipline) / 04 (runtime config) | **L** — narrowed from a standing cache exposure to a single function-call window | OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet |
| 5 | Connector used as a credential-stuffing oracle against the vendor (rate-limit granularity unstated) | No per-identity/per-IP rate-limit granularity stated in the original design | M | M | **Control:** rate limit keyed per calling-API-client *and* per source IP, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth *failure ratio* specifically (credential stuffing shows as a high failure ratio before high volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted, not just per client — closes the granularity gap without adding a new requirement beyond what §4 already gestured at. | 03 (rate-limit logic) / 04 (shared rate-limit store or edge mechanism, if enforced at a gateway) | **L** | OWASP API4:2023; NIST SP 800-63B §5.2.2 |

Rules of use followed: Tomasz ranked and handed over his top five before Helena answered (per `CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩); Felix's alternatives turn sat between red and blue and eliminated leaves 1's storage variant and 2's substrate by design change rather than control; Helena answered every leaf with a control or a justified accepted risk; Tomasz's rebuttal turn on the top three surfaced one unaddressed variant (leaf 1's connection-reuse case) and one boundary needing explicit statement (leaf 3), both resolved without reopening the ruling; Marcus assigned Residual with the justification in each row. No Residual **H** — none of the five leaves cleared that bar after blue's response and the rebuttal-turn tightening, so no follow-up Story is required; leaf 3's **M** is the one residual worth Warren's attention as a genuine accepted risk, not a gap.

Model: sonnet (Tomasz Wrede, red; Felix Adebayo, alternatives; Helena Marsh, blue) / sonnet (Marcus Ilori, lead, ruling and residual scoring). Turns consumed: 5 (red top-five, alternatives, blue, rebuttal, lead residual scoring) — matches the 4-turns-plus-lead budget in `PLAN.md` §3.

## S7a. Token issuance: real `/auth/token` grant logic (LT-51)

**Status: new** — created from Tobias's (01) objection during LT-40's refinement (`01-product-industry-research-design/refinement/LT-40.md`), which found the gap was unscheduled, not pending: LT-32 built `/auth/token`'s route (a 501 placeholder on the issuer Deployment) and LT-40 builds the verifier side, but nothing implemented the grant itself. Without it, "done = deployed and reviewable at a public URL" would have quietly weakened into "reviewable by an engineer with cluster access" — a materially different and less credible bar than the one this project has held since LT-34.

**Description:** OAuth2 client-credentials grant at `POST /auth/token` on the issuer Deployment (`APP_MODE=issuer`), per Marcus's `handoff-03-auth.md` v4 addendum:
- **Client authentication:** HTTP Basic, `client_id`/`client_secret`, form-encoded `client_credentials` grant per RFC 6749 §4.4.
- **Client store:** the issuer's own datastore (not 05's schema — same F8 boundary as `caller_referral`), secrets hashed with Argon2id, never stored or logged in plaintext.
- **Responses:** RFC 6749 §5.1 (success) / §5.2 (error) shape. An unknown `client_id` and a wrong secret for a known `client_id` return the **identical** `invalid_client` error — no oracle letting a caller distinguish "doesn't exist" from "exists, wrong secret."
- **Token:** short-lived RS256 JWT, `iss`/`aud`/`scope`/`exp`/`sub` claims per `handoff-03-auth.md` v3, algorithm/`kid` header per RFC 8725 pinning (matches LT-40's verifier-side allowlist). A `jti` claim is recommended for audit correlation only — **not** a revocation mechanism; TTL-only revocation stands per `api-auth-design.md`, not re-litigated here.
- **Public-key distribution:** a JWKS endpoint served by the issuer's in-cluster `ClusterIP` `Service` — **no public `Ingress` route** for it; only the verifying middleware inside the cluster (LT-40) needs it, and exposing it publicly is unnecessary attack surface for a machine-to-machine API with no external relying parties.

**Acceptance criteria:**
- [ ] `POST /auth/token` implements RFC 6749 §4.4 client-credentials grant with HTTP Basic client auth and form-encoded body, without re-deriving 02's scheme reasoning in `handoff-03-auth.md` v4.
- [ ] Client secrets are stored Argon2id-hashed in the issuer's own datastore; never logged, never returned in any response, never present in plaintext outside the initial provisioning step — enforcement site: log-call review against `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list, plus a test asserting the stored representation isn't the plaintext secret.
- [ ] An unknown `client_id` and a wrong secret for a known `client_id` return byte-identical `invalid_client` error responses — enforcement site: a test comparing both response bodies for equality, not just "both return 401."
- [ ] The issued JWT's claims (`iss`, `aud`, `scope`, `exp`, `sub`) and algorithm/`kid` header match what LT-40's verifier middleware expects and validates — enforcement site: an integration test that mints a token here and verifies it through LT-40's actual middleware, not two independently-trusted unit tests that never touch each other.
- [ ] The JWKS endpoint is served only on the issuer's in-cluster `ClusterIP` `Service`, with no `Ingress` route — enforcement site: manifest inspection (04's side) plus a test confirming the endpoint exists on the issuer's internal address.
- [ ] **A dedicated, narrowly-scoped QA client credential is seeded in the client store specifically for the PO's live-URL review** (LT-34/LT-39/LT-40's joint acceptance) — provisioned out-of-band (same discipline as LT-32's signing-key `Secret`, never through the CI-applied manifest set), scoped to the minimum grant needed to exercise `profile:search`/`profile:read:own`/`profile:read:any`, and named/owned so it can be rotated or revoked independently of any real caller's credential.
- [ ] This story's own failed-grant rate limiting/backoff (LT-40's criterion 5) applies to this endpoint's real implementation, not just the placeholder — confirmed by re-running LT-40's rate-limit tests against the real grant logic, not assumed to still hold.

## S8. Connector: /auth + /identity (Q3) (LT-41)

**Description:** `cmd/idp-connector`'s outbound client and inbound handlers for the assignment's fixed `/auth` and `/identity` contracts, per `connector-security.md`'s no-cache-by-default token lifecycle (fetch per `/identity` call, discard on return; fresh isolated `Authorization` header per outbound request, never inherited from pooling/keep-alive; fetch → use-once → zeroize, retries re-fetch via `/auth`). Includes the connector's own inbound self-auth (distinct audience `idp-connector-service`, scope `connector:identity-lookup`, restricted to `api-service` as caller) per `handoff-03-auth.md` v3 §"Where it's validated" (Ingrid's S8 catch), and the `context.WithTimeout` + bounded pool + backoff requirement from `decisions/go-layout-debate.md`.

**Acceptance criteria:**
- [ ] Implements 02's token-lifecycle spec (`connector-security.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`) without re-litigating the no-cache-by-default posture.
- [ ] No vendor token is persisted or cached across requests by default; if a future exception is ever justified it requires re-review, not a silent default change — enforcement site: no storage/cache call in the token-fetch path.
- [ ] Every outbound vendor call sets `context.WithTimeout`; the outbound HTTP client uses a bounded connection pool; token-refresh retries back off rather than tight-looping — enforcement site: the HTTP client construction plus a test asserting a timeout fires.
- [ ] The connector's own `/auth`/`/identity` handlers validate an inbound JWT with `aud: idp-connector-service` and `scope: connector:identity-lookup`, distinct from `api-service`'s audience — enforcement site: this handler's own middleware instance, with a test asserting a `loginid-api-service`-audience token is rejected here.
- [ ] Never logs, in any environment or error path: the end-user's vendor password, any vendor `access_token` or full `Authorization` header value, or raw vendor error response bodies (per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list) — enforcement site: log-call review against that list.

**Depends on:** S1; **02: Connector security spec** (`connector-security.md`, accepted); **02: Auth scheme and authorization hand-off** (`handoff-03-auth.md` v3, accepted, for the connector's own self-auth).
**Model/effort:** opus, high — the only implementation seat at this tier, per `planning-approach.md`'s connector-token-lifecycle exception.
**Type:** Story.
**Labels:** `track-03`, `security-graded`, `no-code-yet`.

package api

import (
	"context"
	"sync"
	"time"
)

// RateLimiter enforces handoff-03-auth.md v3's per-client-credential
// rate limits (60 req/min for read:own/read:any, 10 req/min for
// profile:search), keyed per sub+scope. The mechanism is this track's
// call (v3, explicitly) — an interface so handler tests use a fake
// instead of real wall-clock behavior.
type RateLimiter interface {
	// Allow reports whether sub may make one more request under scope
	// right now, given limit requests per rolling minute.
	Allow(ctx context.Context, sub string, scope Scope, limit int) (bool, error)
}

// InProcessRateLimiter is a fixed-window (per-wall-clock-minute) limiter,
// in-process rather than Redis-backed — the simplest mechanism satisfying
// v3's "the mechanism is your call" framing, sufficient for this
// take-home's single-instance deployment. A fixed window (vs. sliding) can
// admit up to 2x limit across a window boundary; accepted here as a
// deliberate simplification, not hidden — a production multi-instance
---replicas---

# Deploy manifests for LT-34/LT-45/LT-47's vertical slice, extended in
# LT-32 for the issuer Deployment split (F13's fix) and LT-52 for a real
# Postgres instance, the migration Job, and DB_DSN_FILE wiring into
# api-service. No CockroachDB deployment here — CI-only (LT-47), per
# LT-52's stated scope limit.
#
# No Namespace object here, deliberately: the scoped loginid-takehome-deployer
# ServiceAccount's RBAC (deploy/rbac.yaml) is namespace-scoped to
# loginid-takehome and correctly cannot get/create/patch a cluster-scoped
# Namespace object — that's the isolation working as designed, not a gap.
# The namespace itself is created once, out of band, by whoever runs the
# initial cluster bootstrap (deploy/rbac.yaml also creates
# loginid-takehome-runners for the same reason).
#
# No Secret object here either, and none ever will be for the JWT signing
# key specifically: deploy/rbac.yaml's Role grants nothing on `secrets` at
# all, so an attempt to apply one here would fail Forbidden, the same way
# Namespace did. The signing-key Secret is created out-of-band — see
# deploy/issuer-secret-bootstrap.yaml — per Tobias's (01) accepted
# objection on LT-32's refinement: the CI-applied manifest set must never
# be the thing that can read or write that key.

58:**Finding: the CI acceptance criterion is not met, and "Build, lint, test" going green does not mean what it appears to mean.** `newPostgresFamilyFixture` (shared by the `postgres` and `cockroachdb` backends) explicitly skips (`t.Skipf`) when its DSN env var (`TEST_POSTGRES_DSN`/`TEST_COCKROACHDB_DSN`) isn't set — by its own comment, "this suite asserts behavior, it doesn't stand up infrastructure." `pr-check.yml` never sets either variable and never provisions a Postgres/CockroachDB service or testcontainer. Only the SQLite fixture is genuinely self-contained (in-memory). **Net effect: every Postgres and CockroachDB conformance test — including `TestConformance_CockroachDB_RealSerializationRetry`, this story's own flagship acceptance criterion — silently skips on every CI run, and Go reports a skip as neither pass nor fail, so nothing currently flags this as a problem.** `go test ./... -race` returning success proves SQLite's conformance tests passed; it proves nothing about the other two backends.
60:**Ruling (team-lead):** the suite itself is well-built and correctly scoped — this is a pipeline-wiring gap, not a defect in the tests. Fix in flight: Theo adds real Postgres/CockroachDB service containers to `pr-check.yml`; Renata makes the fixtures fail loudly under `CI=true` rather than skip, so a future regression of this exact gap can't hide behind a green build again.
---LT-39---
34:- No rate-limit/counter *mechanism* choice (Redis vs. in-process) — that's Renata's implementation call, per v3's own "the mechanism is your call" framing.
82:**Real defect found and reproduced live, not from code alone: the per-minute `RateLimiter` (10 req/min search) does not reliably enforce its stated limit.** `api-service` runs 2 replicas; `RateLimiter` is in-process (per-pod memory) — its own code comment states this is "a deliberate simplification, not hidden — a production multi-instance deployment would need a shared store (Redis) instead," but the deployed manifest runs 2 replicas anyway. Reproduced: a 12-call rapid burst produced zero 429s (load-balanced roughly evenly across both pods, each staying under its own independent count of 10); a subsequent 30-call burst started producing 429s at call 9, at a point with no fixed relationship to the stated "10 req/min." The effective limit is somewhere between 10–20 depending on load-balancer distribution, not a real per-client cap. `TouchCounter` and `GrantLimiter` share the identical in-process, per-pod design (confirmed by reading their source) and almost certainly have the same gap, though I could not exercise `TouchCounter`'s cap live — see below.
86:**Ruling (team-lead):** `api-service` moves from 2 replicas to 1 (matching the issuer Deployment's own replica count) — a security control beats nominal HA for a POC at this scale; Redis remains the documented production-scale path (already stated in the code's own comments), not something to build now. Re-run the rate-limit burst test after Theo's manifest PR redeploys.
88:**Correction (Tobias's objection, verified before applying) — 1 replica alone does not close this, it only narrows it, and the doc must say so plainly.** Confirmed directly: neither `deploy/manifests.yaml` nor `deploy/api-service.yaml` sets a `Deployment.spec.strategy` field, so Kubernetes' default applies — `RollingUpdate` with `maxSurge: 25%`, which rounds up to 1 additional pod even at `replicas: 1`. Every future merge-to-main deploy (LT-47's auto-deploy-on-merge design) briefly runs **two** `api-service` pods simultaneously while the new one's readiness probe is pending — the exact condition that broke the limiter in this review's live test, recurring on a predictable schedule during every deploy, not eliminated by the replica-count change. **This is a partial mitigation, not a fix.** The only complete closure is the shared store (Redis) the code's own comment already names as the multi-instance answer. Counter-proposal accepted: flagging to Theo that `api-service`'s `Deployment.spec.strategy.type` should be set to `Recreate` (guaranteeing exactly one pod at any time, at the cost of a brief availability gap during deploy — the same trade this ruling already accepted the spirit of, now actually holding through a rollout too), as a cheap second step beyond the replica-count change alone.
92:- Per-minute `RateLimiter` (10 req/min search / 60 req/min read) — **verified by unit test only; live verification pending the replica fix.** The reproduced cross-replica gap above is real and was live, but it's a deployment-topology defect the replica-count ruling addresses, not a defect in the limiter's own logic (which the unit tests already correctly cover for a single instance).

Two independent in-process counters behind one Service means a caller distributed round-robin across both pods sees roughly double the stated per-minute cap before either counter individually trips — the reasoning holds without needing to reproduce the burst myself, since it follows directly from "in-process, per-pod state" + "2 replicas" as a matter of how a fixed-window in-memory counter works, independent of any live timing. **One thing worth flagging back, already caught in your own record:** I read `refinement/LT-39.md`'s later entries and Tobias's objection is exactly right and worth taking seriously — dropping to 1 replica doesn't fully close this, because Kubernetes' default `RollingUpdate` strategy (`maxSurge: 25%` → rounds up to 1 extra pod) means every future merge-to-main deploy briefly runs 2 `api-service` pods again during rollout, recreating the identical gap on a predictable schedule. 11:1. **Interface drift is silent with hand-written fakes.** A new interface method still compiles against a stale fake, which silently zero-values it instead of failing a test that should catch the gap. Generated mocks fail loudly or leave a visibly regenerated stub. Marisol's own mitigation: enforce fake regeneration in the same PR as any interface change, as a review checklist item — makes drift a procedural risk, not a tooling one.
21:Adopted: any change to `Repository` or its three sub-interfaces must update the corresponding hand-written fake in the same PR — added as a stated review-checklist item for S2/S5, not left as an assumed discipline. This is the review-time equivalent of what a generated mock would catch automatically, at zero added dependency cost.

13	func TestNewHTTPVendorClient_RejectsNonHTTPS(t *testing.T) {
14		if _, err := NewHTTPVendorClient("http://vendor.example.com", nil); err == nil {
15			t.Errorf("NewHTTPVendorClient accepted a plain http:// base URL, want an error (connector-security.md §2: TLS only)")
16		}

18			t.Errorf("NewHTTPVendorClient accepted a garbage base URL, want an error")
19		}

21	
22	// TestHTTPVendorClient_FetchIdentity_FreshAuthorizationHeaderPerRequest
23	// is attack-tree leaf 1's own named enforcement site (refinement/LT-41.md):
24	// two sequential calls through the SAME pooled *http.Client, each
25	// presenting a DIFFERENT vendor token, must each produce an outbound
26	// Authorization header matching ITS OWN token — never the previous
27	// call's, which a client that held a shared/default header (instead of
28	// building it fresh per call) could otherwise leak across requests via
29	// connection reuse.
30	func TestHTTPVendorClient_FetchIdentity_FreshAuthorizationHeaderPerRequest(t *testing.T) {
31		var mu sync.Mutex
32		var observedHeaders []string
33	
34		srv := httptest.NewServer(http.HandlerFunc(func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
35			mu.Lock()
36			observedHeaders = append(observedHeaders, r.Header.Get("Authorization"))
37			mu.Unlock()
38			w.Header().Set("Content-Type", "application/json")
39			_ = json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(vendorIdentityResponse{Name: "ok"})
40		}))
41		defer srv.Close()
42	
43		// Construct directly (bypassing NewHTTPVendorClient's https-only
44		// check, which httptest.Server's plain-http URL would otherwise
45		// fail) — this file is in the same package, so it can set the
46		// unexported fields directly rather than needing a second,
47		// test-only constructor in non-test code.
48		client := &HTTPVendorClient{baseURL: srv.URL, httpClient: srv.Client()}
49	
50		if _, err := client.FetchIdentity(context.Background(), "token-A", "+15555550100", "Jane"); err != nil {
51			t.Fatalf("first FetchIdentity call: %v", err)
52		}
53		if _, err := client.FetchIdentity(context.Background(), "token-B", "+15555550100", "Jane"); err != nil {
54			t.Fatalf("second FetchIdentity call: %v", err)
55		}
56	
57		if len(observedHeaders) != 2 {
58			t.Fatalf("server observed %d requests, want 2", len(observedHeaders))
59		}
60		if observedHeaders[0] != "Bearer token-A" {
61			t.Errorf("first request's Authorization = %q, want %q", observedHeaders[0], "Bearer token-A")
62		}
63		if observedHeaders[1] != "Bearer token-B" {
64			t.Errorf("second request's Authorization = %q, want %q — must not inherit the first call's token via connection reuse", observedHeaders[1], "Bearer token-B")
65		}
66	}

package connector

import (
	"context"
	"encoding/json"
	"io"
	"net"
	"net/http"
	"strings"
	"time"

	"loginid-takehome/internal/api"

// identityFailureFloor is /identity's uniform response-time floor across
// its three failure branches (no-token, bad-token, vendor-rejected) —
// Marcus Ilori's (02) ruling on Tobias Lindqvist's timing-oracle
// objection (refinement/LT-41.md), recorded in full in
// connector-security.md §4. ~100ms, well under any context.WithTimeout
// budget elsewhere in the chain (vendorCallTimeout is 10s) so it never
// competes with those deadlines. This narrows, but does not fully close,
// the gap between a local-only failure (microseconds) and a
// vendor-round-trip failure (tens-hundreds of ms) — full closure is an
// accepted non-goal per that ruling.
const identityFailureFloor = 100 * time.Millisecond

// identityErrorBody is the one JSON shape every /identity failure
// returns, regardless of which of the three branches produced it —
// content-identical by construction (a single writeIdentityFailure call
// site below), not by three call sites happening to independently agree.
type identityErrorBody struct {
	Error string `json:"error"`
}

// vendorLimiter is the interface these handlers depend on — satisfied
// directly by api.GrantLimiter (PR #30/#34's already-built component,
// reused here rather than rebuilt per LT-41's own non-goals: "this
// story's own in-process rate limiting is sufficient, matching the
// pattern already used for RateLimiter/TouchCounter/GrantLimiter").
// Declared locally so this package's own tests can supply a minimal fake
// satisfying only the calls actually made here, per
// decisions/test-double-strategy.md.
type vendorLimiter interface {
	Allow(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string) (bool, error)
	RecordFailure(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string)
	RecordSuccess(ctx context.Context, clientID, sourceIP string)
}

// Deps bundles NewAuthHandler/NewIdentityHandler's collaborators.
//
// IdentityLimiter is the second rate-limit dimension attack-tree leaf 5
// actually specifies ("per identity attempted, not just per client") —
// Limiter alone (keyed by calling-API-client sub + source IP) means one
// success anywhere resets that caller's whole consecutive-failure count,
// letting a compromised caller cycle through many stolen identities
// indefinitely (Tomasz Wrede's cold review, PR #40). May be nil in tests
// that don't exercise this dimension; NewConnectorRouter always
// constructs a real one.
type Deps struct {
	Vendor          VendorClient
